It also gives an exact *per-context* test by a sequence of finite computads whose object cycles use only edges forced to have weight zero. For Q3(a), these finite intervals behave differently from finite-sheeted covers: there are old 4-types that have compatible strict identity witnesses on **every** finite cover, even though a single finite interval proves them raw-empty.

### The finite computad for a boundary

Assign a vector $w(e)\in\mathbb N^k$ to each generating arrow of a finite computad $X$. Require that the two attaching paths of each generating 2-cell have the same weight, using their flattened words. This gives every higher declaration a weight, the weight of either exterior 1-pole, since the grading takes values in the one-object category of $\mathbb N^k$ with only identities above dimension one. In particular no homogeneity hypothesis on the *number* of 2- or 3-events in a higher attachment is needed.

For $b\in\mathbb N^k$ put an object $(x,i)$ at each old vertex $x$ and each integral $0\le i\le b$, coordinatewise. For each positive-dimensional declaration $g$ put one declaration $g_i$ for each $0\le i\le b-w(g)$. Its exterior poles are $(s_0 g,i)$ and $(t_0 g,i+w(g))$. Use the original written attaching terms, placing each label at its actual starting coordinate. Call the result $X[b]$. For example, a 2-generator replacing $ab$ by $c$ with weights $1,2,3$ has source the composite of $a_i$ and $b_{i+1}$ and target $c_i$. A generator from an empty path to a zero-weight loop is available at every coordinate. This includes all weak spellings in the attachments.

Given an old type $A$ of dimension at least two, first compare the weights of its two positive-dimensional faces. If they differ, ordinary strict soundness excludes a filler on every surface. Otherwise let $b$ be that weight. Place the type at coordinate zero in $X[b]$. In each individual theory its inhabitation is equivalent to inhabitation of $A$ in $X$. More generally the projection $X[B]\to X$ reflects and preserves inhabitation at **every** old boundary of rank at least two in $X[B]$, for any box $B$. Thus $X$ is conservative through a given rank, or in all ranks, exactly when every $X[B]$ is. This last equivalence uses boxes over the original coloured object set.

To prove this, first use the unrestricted cover with coordinates in $\mathbb Z^k$. Starting at coordinate $i$, an arrow of weight $a$ ends at $i+a$. A tuple on a pasting tree fixes the increment in every outer column because all arrow labels there are joined by 2-labels. Starting at any specified vertex of the spine, add the increments going right and subtract them going left. Each variable of the tuple now has its starting coordinate. Substitution respects placement since an interval in a substituted spine has increment the sum of its subdividing increments. This constructs the lifted attaching terms in declaration order and lifts a raw derivation at a prescribed starting coordinate. The fold followed by anchored lifting recovers a writing upstairs at its original coordinates. For surface derivations use the anchored lifting of round-1 Response 20: the word and head equations use the same placed input positions, disc and endo removal retain the placed entry and face, identity pruning has zero increment on a contracted arrow, and insertion uses the subdivided list of increments at the splice. This argument only uses the group law on grades and the unchanged bound-arity side condition. It applies to $E$ because ordinary strict projection gives the grade and the bound head, with its chosen entry, is the same head before and after placing the tuple. At rank two the specified paths fix the grade as well as the source coordinate; this is the reason for the rank bound. [Anchored lifting under conversion](../answered/semistrict-surface-conservativity-2026-11-24.md#lifting-through-a-graph-of-object-identifications)

Here is why the **finite** box still suffices, including for a derivation using conversion intermediates. In coordinate $j$, give heights below the box, in the box, and above the box the zone numbers $0,1,2$. On vertices of the unrestricted cover add these $k$ numbers. Give every edge the difference of that sum between its endpoints. Each difference is nonnegative by monotonicity. A declaration has difference zero precisely when both endpoints are in the same zone in *each* coordinate. Its attaching terms stay within those zones: in a raw head, the ordinary total traverses its outer columns, so each label lies between the two exterior coordinates. Induct on the raw spelling. The zero-difference declarations are therefore a subcomputad, a disjoint union of pieces with the box itself one piece.

The proof of Response 22 applies also to adding higher declarations of positive difference. In its model keep the free model of the zero-difference subcomputad at weight zero. For each additional generator assign the unique positive-weight cell at the already assigned boundary. Both 1-poles have its specified weight. The same head-sum and equation checks give a model of the selected surface. It follows that a filler at an old boundary in the box gives a filler in the zero-difference subcomputad. Send every other piece by a raw substitution to a point in the box, fixing the box. Such a substitution is built in declaration order: over the free point every prescribed positive-dimensional raw type has a coherence with the required identity strict value. This respects compound attachments literally. Only finitely many declarations occur in a proof. Conversely a box filler projects directly. For a query originally in $X[B]$ at coordinate $i$, anchor the unrestricted lift of any filler of its projection at $i$; its prescribed old faces are exactly the ones in $X[B]$. This proves the per-context assertion. [Positive-weight model](#researcher-response-22)

There is an effective strongest simultaneous test of this kind by *additive* edge weights. Form the integer matrix whose rows are the differences of the arrow occurrence vectors at the two ends of each 2-generator. The allowed scalar gradings form the cone $w\ge0$, $Mw=0$. Use primitive integer generators of its extreme rays as the coordinates. They can be found by enumerating subsets of columns: on the support of an extreme ray the restricted nullspace has dimension one and contains a strictly positive vector. Indeed a second independent direction permits two sufficiently small opposite perturbations within the same support. Conversely any nonnegative decomposition of a positive vector on such a one-dimensional support stays on that support. Every nonzero grading is a nonnegative combination of these rays, by taking the section $\sum w_e=1$, a bounded rational polytope. With a zero cone use the zero grading. Consequently an edge stays at one coordinate exactly when **every** conserved nonnegative grading gives it weight zero. Any directed cycle in a box consists entirely of such edges. If a strictly positive edge grading exists, the boxes have acyclic object graphs, even if the original computad has loops and arbitrarily long paths. The test allows scalar declarations of weight zero at every level. For an individual boundary it can be combined directly with the through-degree-four variable-face criterion of Response 5 or the degree-five one-sided criterion of Response 14 on the finite box. ### Why all finite sheets can miss the directed obstruction

Use the admissible kernel $E'$ of Response 22, obtained from $E$ by guarding the Gray inner identities by a return arrow. Start with its example $X^+$ on $x,y$ with a compound 3-attachment, four forward arrows and the return $y\to x$. At the old reverse $u\to v$, give the forward arrows weight zero and the return weight one. Its zero box is exactly the original two-object computad before adjoining the return. A substitution from that box to the bare suspended square rules out the strict reverse by requirement 3. Thus a finite box actually detects the failure of strict reachability there. Its projection proves absence of a raw or interchange-free converted filler in $X^+$ by the theorem above and strict soundness on the box.

By a finite-sheeted cover here mean a cover of the object graph with bijective transport of sheets along each arrow, carrying the lifts of the attaching declarations at every sheet with their specified boundaries. In any such cover of a strongly connected object graph, **every directed path upstairs has a return**. Lift a base path back, then repeat the resulting base cycle. Its action on the finite starting fibre is a permutation, so after finitely many repetitions one returns to the original sheet. Hence each connected component upstairs is strongly connected. Its free $E'$-structure is the Gray quotient by Response 22. At every lift of the old boundary the two schedules therefore agree by strict inner interchange; their identity witnesses are compatible under all covering maps. Projection of a raw or interchange-free surface filler would give a forbidden one downstairs. Even the simultaneous test of all these compatible strict witnesses gives the wrong inhabitation answer.

Explicitly, for every $m\ge1$ take one copy of the original attachment at each pair $x_i,y_i$, indices modulo $m$, and put the return from $y_i$ to $x_{i+1}$. The shortest nonempty directed object cycle has length $2m$. Thus arbitrarily large finite directed girth does not repair the strict test. The same argument works with the bare globular suspended square in place of the compound attachment. In that variant each finite cover is a globular set of generators, so the variable $A,U$ surface is conservative there in **all** degrees by Response 5, while the strict test still fails in degree four. Suspending the initial square before adjoining the return puts the discrepancy at any prescribed rank at least four. This distinguishes a failure of the strict test from a failure of surface conservativity.

**Validation.** Run `python3 tools/semistrict_surface_weighted_arity_cover.py --agda-dir /tmp/catt-weight-cover` and check `WeightedArityCover.agda` with `agda --safe -i src -i /tmp/catt-weight-cover /tmp/catt-weight-cover/WeightedArityCover.agda`. The checker constructs the box presentations, infers the placed terms at their literal boundaries and folds 487 placements back to their original terms. The tests include scalar and three-coordinate gradings, a positive-width compound attachment and a zero-width birth and death. Agda checks four multigraded placements and both schedules in a cyclic cover. The checker also constructs four cyclic covers of the compound attachment, recovers its exact zero box, checks the native bare-square direction, and tests the directed return property on 14,049 finite graph covers. ## Researcher Response 29

**All ten individual ordinary surfaces are conservative through degree five in every finite computad whose generating 2-cells have single-edge spatial input and single-edge spatial output.** The two edges may differ, the written poles need only have these strict words, and all declarations above dimension two are arbitrary. In particular a 3-rule can replace several unary gate strings on several wires simultaneously, or can be a 3-scalar on an empty word. A degree-five failure for an individual surface requires a generating 2-cell with an empty or multi-edge pole on the support of the failure.

There is also an all-dimensional reduction on the 3-skeleton. Two substitutions jointly detect higher raw boundary spheres: erase the empty-word 3-generators, or flatten all unary 2-generators. Thus on this class of 3-skeleta it suffices, for each individual surface in degrees six and above, to settle its positive case and its graph-based case. Here are the fixed-hom calculations and the extra argument for arbitrary higher declarations in degree five.

### What the two projections remember

Write $B$ for the 3-skeleton, $B^+$ for the part retaining the nonempty-word 3-rules, and $B^f$ for the graph-based 3-computad obtained by identifying edge names connected by a unary gate, suppressing the gates and keeping a separate 3-generator for each old 3-rule on its flattened word. Write $B^f_+$ for its nonempty-word part. A unary gate changes a single edge between the same objects: every 2-diagram has a fixed number of lanes, with a directed word of unary gates on each. A rule's two strict 2-faces therefore have the same flattened word; every nonempty-word marker has equally many input and output ports in its lower circuit. The erasures to the two presentations exist on the actual raw generators. Fit the graph-only images to the written faces by graph comparisons. An empty-word 3-generator has vertex-only faces, so it can be assigned a comparison there when erased. The square down to $B^f_+$ commutes on the marked transition presentation, with the same lower fits up to contractible choices.

Fix the two actual lower diagrams and give separate names to the occurrences of a strict 3-label. In a serial history each nonempty-word event (a rod) occupies a fixed interval of **lane indices**, and a scalar occupies a fixed gap. Boundary fitting uses the bubble-free unary 2-computad. A rod interval is preserved across a temporal exchange: both faces of every marker preserve all the lane indices. Scalar positions are independent of whether a unary gate has changed the name on an adjacent edge. The strict label consists of its label in $B^+$ and its multiset of positioned scalars. Indeed one can place all the scalars last; at any adjacent rod the two marked rows can be stacked in a lower circuit, and scalar-scalar exchanges exist too. The two projections preserve these data. In particular identifying edge colours in $B^f$ does not itself identify positions of repeated visits to a vertex.

At these named labels let $E,E_+,E_f,E_{f,+}$ denote the raw mapping spaces in the four presentations. Then
$$
                     E\ \simeq\ E_+\times^{h}_{E_{f,+}} E_f.                 \tag{1}
$$
The map is given by the two erasures. The base $E_{f,+}$ is the aspherical interval arrangement of Response 19. In the flat space $E_f$, deletion of the scalars is the configuration bundle of Response 23. In particular it has connected fibres and is surjective on based fundamental groups. The gate labels and gate strings can impose dependencies in $E_+$; asphericity of that space is unnecessary.

### Lower charts and temporal face maps

Use the space-valued change-index presentation of Response 21. Its horizontal flags are still discrete paths, with the bubble-free unary base at change count zero. Horizontal localization at index $k$ adjoins the marked 2-corollas at their spatial paths; the planar tier model computes its hom spaces even at a floating scalar. At change count zero in the innermost direction the original unary 2-diagrams have contractible components. The same Segal product and completeness check therefore applies. With distinct names the extreme hom attaches a permutohedron per temporal block with coefficient the space of lower marked diagrams. In the positive presentation the coefficients have contractible components, giving the usual complex of strings of marked circuits. [Space-valued attachment](#why-chart-components-suffice-for-4-tracks) · [Positive transition presentation](../answered/relative-coherence-all-dimensions-r6-2026-11-20.md#a-presentation-of-the-geometric-fibre)

Fix a positive lower circuit in a block and insert scalar markers at prescribed gaps. Its stationary ordinary gates lie on single lanes. The rods straddling a gap are totally ordered along either adjacent wire. Their order splits the gap into the possible regions for a scalar in that circuit. No ordinary gate straddles the gap. More precisely, use vertical coordinates $y$ for the ordinary unary vertices and all markers, with strict inequalities in succession on each wire. For scalars in the same gap retain horizontal coordinates $x$ and require distinct $(y,x)$; for a scalar and a rod straddling it require distinct $y$. The weak orders of $y$ with the orders of tied points in a gap give exactly the planar tiers, since all other horizontal orders in a row are forced by the occupied lanes. The order-poset comparison of Response 23 identifies this space with the lower chart (all the allowed scalar placements); ordinary vertices are already distinguished by lane and index between consecutive rods.

Forgetting the stationary $y$ coordinates is an equivalence onto the corresponding flat chart over the given order of rods on each lane. Between successive rods on a lane insert its specified string of ordinary gates at equally spaced heights. Before the first and after the last use consecutive exterior heights; if the lane has no rod use any fixed increasing list. These choices give a continuous section. The fibre of forgotten coordinates is convex and the straight-line homotopy stays in it. A passage from one wire to another in the lower order must pass through a rod, so there are no further inequalities to impose after erasure. The actual flattening morphism of planar charts induces this forgetful map: on tier posets it erases the stationary vertices and suppresses empty rows. The coordinate comparison uses restriction of weak orders, hence intertwines the maps. This proof includes coincident-gap scalar pairs with two horizontal orders.

At a temporal split, a removed rod is expanded in one factor to its actual source circuit and in the other to its actual target circuit. Those circuits contain only unary vertices. Erasing them gives precisely the flat split by removal of that rod. Existing scalar gaps and orders relative to surviving straddlers are unchanged. Thus the lower equivalences above are objectwise equivalences of the coefficient diagrams under temporal splitting. One can use the erasure on the actual marked meshes for the natural map; the equally spaced section proves each equivalence individually and need not commute with expansion.

### Why the pullback is homotopically correct

Here are details concerning simultaneous orders. They matter because taking the strict pullback of arbitrary chart complexes need not compute (1). Let $C$ be the positive temporal chart poset at the named positive label, with arrows splitting tiers. An element remembers the positive marked circuit in every tier, including its stationary gate words. Enlarge $C$ by choosing a global weak order $Y$ on the imaginary coordinates of the rods, strict in the prescribed direction on every pair of temporally tied rods sharing a lane. Order the enlarged poset by temporal refinement and refinement of $Y$. Its projection to $C$ is an equivalence on nerves: it is the Grothendieck poset of the allowed $Y$-orders with inclusion along a split. Each such order poset stratifies a nonempty convex region, as in Response 19. Existence follows by serializing each positive circuit. Denote the enlarged poset by $C'$. It maps to $F$, the flat poset of allowed pairs of weak orders $(t,y)$ on rods (two rods on a common wire cannot tie in both).

Let $F'$ be the flat poset also retaining all scalars, with weak orders $(t,y,x)$ as in Response 23, $x$ on scalars only. Then the nerve of $C'\times_F F'$ computes $E$. To see the indexing explicitly, choose a temporal partition of all names. On the induced nonempty rod tiers choose a positive chart in $C$. An intervening scalar-only tier uses the stationary positive diagram at that cut, uniquely determined by the neighbouring positive histories. On each tier the preceding lower calculation replaces the coefficient by its allowed flat $(y,x)$ data with the positive rod orders. Taking global weak orders instead of separate per-tier orders subdivides the product of the coordinate fans. Splitting a temporal tier relaxes the corresponding inequalities. The nerve of the Grothendieck poset for these choices is their homotopy colimit, exactly the space obtained by the temporal permutohedral attachment. Its objects and refinements are the indicated poset pullback. This also identifies the two comparison maps on paths and on higher spheres.

The projection $NF'\to NF$ computes homotopy pullbacks after arbitrary simplicial base change. For detail, over any nondegenerate simplex of $NF$, a flag of base orders, its simplicial preimage is the nerve of the subposet of $F'$ projecting into that flag. In real coordinates this computes the preimage of the union of the relatively open base fan faces in the flag. Indeed for any union of such open faces barycentric normalization onto the allowed face poset gives a homotopy equivalence: in a subdivision simplex renormalize its weights on the allowed vertices; the largest face with positive weight is allowed and remains present throughout. Use the radial link of the pointed fan after splitting off translations, with the origin included if appropriate. The projection of an open coordinate-order face is the indicated open base face. The coordinate preimage is consequently a union of whole faces, to which the same argument applies. The inclusion for a subflag is respected by the barycentric face models (use their inclusions back into the coordinate spaces). Each base flag union is contractible. The actual coordinate map deleting scalars is the locally trivial bundle of Response 23, since straddlers are always distinct punctures. It follows by the bundle sequence that every subflag inclusion induces a weak equivalence on the preimages. Degenerate flags repeat a stage and retract by removing the repetitions. This simplex criterion gives the asserted homotopy pullback property: resolve a simplicial base by its simplices, take the homotopy colimit of the preimages, and use that every face and degeneracy acts by an equivalence on their homotopy types. The resolution realizes to the actual pullback (the simplex over each of its simplices has a terminal factorization), and its fibre at a vertex has that preimage's homotopy type. Applying this to $NC'\to NF$ proves (1). The construction is natural in the erasures. [Flat fan and bundle](#configuration-space-for-a-named-label) · [Interval arrangement](#researcher-response-19)

Since the common base is a 1-type, (1) detects every based higher sphere jointly in the two projections. At tracks it gives *all* pairs whose projections agree as path classes at the specified ends: take a homotopy between the two projected paths, and the ambiguity in such a homotopy relative boundary is $\pi_2 E_{f,+}=0$. For old faces with repeated generator names first lift a surface witness and its faces by occurrence splitting. For degree at least six apply joint detection of the resulting raw sphere; for degree five use the track test with the same named ends. Substituting a proposed surface witness gives witnesses of that surface under both erasures. This proves the all-dimensional reduction on $B$. The positive and flat degree-five theorems already settle that degree on $B$ itself.

### The target for arbitrary higher declarations

For the stronger claim on extensions, use the surface target of Response 10 for the positive presentation, the assembled target of Response 27 for the flat presentation, and its central rod-only target for the common flat positive presentation. We detail the comparison needed for base change. Each pullback 3-object has a positive history object and a compatible flat object. Its positive component fixes a positive label. The two tracks now agree in the common target. This gives essential surjectivity, also for binary history objects by full faithfulness of normal evaluation.

Between actual raw ends, a prescribed pullback track fixes the transported scalar names; its interval transports from the two sides agree because each interval is read on at least one wire in the common rod-only groupoid. Distinguish all the names accordingly. The two coordinate tracks are realized as named path classes by Responses 10 and 27. Formula (1) supplies the required joint raw path, and proves injectivity on path classes as well. ## Researcher Response 30

**The degree-five theorem extends to splits, merges and arbitrary positive planar 2-gates, provided no such gate has a scalar-bearing vertex strictly inside either of its two spatial paths.** Precisely, assume the two strict path boundaries of every generating 2-cell are nonempty. Let $P$ be the set of vertices admitting an empty-word 3-generator. For each generating 2-cell, read the sequence of vertices on each boundary, and test its *internal positions* against $P$. If none belong to $P$, all ten individual ordinary surfaces are conservative through degree five, with arbitrary higher declarations. A gate may start or end at a vertex of $P$, including both ends at the same vertex. Generating 3-rules can cross arbitrarily many visits to $P$. The hypothesis is decidable by reading the declarations.

Consequently, on a positive 2-skeleton a degree-five counterexample must use an empty-word 3-generator at a vertex which also occurs strictly inside a spatial path of a generating 2-cell on the support. Merely having both a width-changing gate and a scalar, even in the same diagram and on incident wires, is insufficient. The all-dimensional two-projection reduction of Response 29 holds on the corresponding 3-skeleta.

### Recording the moving cuts

Map each vertex outside $P$ to a single new vertex $*$ and retain each $p\in P$. Introduce edges $L_p:*\to p$ and $R_p:p\to *$. Map an original edge $u\to v$ to $R_u$ if $u$ is protected, followed by $L_v$ if $v$ is protected; omit either missing letter. This is a path substitution. The image of a nonempty path records each internal visit to $p$ by the consecutive pair $L_pR_p$, as well as a right half-edge at a protected start and a left half-edge at a protected end. In particular the protected gaps of the image are in bijection with the actual visits, including for an empty path at $p$. Repeated visits to the same vertex still have different positions.

Both paths of an ordinary gate have just the same exterior letters. Hence the gate goes to an identity in the flat strict presentation. At the raw faces use graph-only fits. Retain a nonempty-word 3-rule as its own flat rod when its spatial image is nonempty; when the image has no letters, assign a graph-only comparison and omit that rod in the flat presentation. In both cases its two spatial words have the same image since they are connected by a lower diagram. Keep each actual scalar at its protected vertex. The resulting flat computad is graph-based, with no scalar at $*$. The other projection just erases original scalar generators and keeps all the original positive rules and gates. Use the common flat rod-only presentation below these two projections. If $P$ is empty, the positive theorem already applies directly.

In every positive lower circuit an individual occurrence of a protected gap can be followed from input word to output word. Across an ordinary row it is outside the rewriting subword or one of its endpoints. At an endpoint it follows the same side of the gate; its numerical index in the current word can change. A 3-marker standing for a change has the same sequence of protected visits on its two spatial paths. When it strictly straddles one of these visits it separates the lower and upper portions of that gap. These are exactly the original markers whose flat intervals strictly straddle the corresponding gap between $L_p$ and $R_p$. For any flat half-edge the retained rods using it are causally ordered in the original lower circuit. Follow the actual wire immediately adjacent on that side of the chosen visit. When a gate or marker meets this wire it has an input and output on that side, giving a directed connection through the box. If the visit passes inside a marker, its two incident wires likewise connect through the box. Positivity supplies both ports throughout. This argument proves, in particular, that flattening an outer exchange of Response 10 gives an independent exchange or an identity.

Partition the 2-generators into **structural gates** and **unary transitions**, the latter between individual parallel edges. Allow three kinds of 3-generator, at arbitrary raw writings of the indicated strict faces.

- A scalar over an empty edge path.

Form a planar 2-computad by identifying edge names connected by unary transitions, suppressing those transitions and identifying structural gate names connected by single-gate changes. ## Researcher Response 32

**A fixed old boundary admits an exact reduction along any category-valued labelling of its edge paths.** Both the prefix and the suffix of a position must be retained. The universal such reduction separates the two object poles by a directed cut exactly when the path of the query is noninvertible in the category presented by the 2-skeleton. This gives a degree-six reflection criterion for Q3(b,c) on cyclic computads, including cases in which every conserved additive edge weight vanishes. For Q1(a) there is a fixed shape of old 4-boundary whose raw and $A,U$ reverse is always absent, but for which detection by strict tests on these reductions is equivalent to a presented-category invertibility problem. Some instances require an infinite category even when **all** finite category labellings are allowed.

### Two-sided placements at a written boundary

Let $C_X$ be the free category on the object graph of a finite computad $X$, subject to the equation between the two edge words of each 2-generator. Use execution order for products. Fix a functor $F:C_X\to C$ to a small category. The two strict edge poles of any cell of degree at least two have the same $C$-arrow. For such an old query with exterior objects $x,y$ call this arrow $w:Fx\to Fy$. Form the following computad $X_w$.

* Over an object $z$ put a copy $(z,l,r)$ for each factorization $l r=w$ through $Fz$.
* A declaration $d$ of positive degree with object poles $z,t$ and arrow value $b:Fz\to Ft$ has one copy for each $l b r=w$. Its exterior objects are $(z,l,br)$ and $(t,lb,r)$. For degree one this gives the attaching type. For higher degrees place both written faces with the same outside factors $l,r$, recursively.

For any term of value $b$ and frame $l,b,r$, placement commutes with taking its typed faces. On a generic head read the outer spine of its tuple. All versions of the edge in a column have the same arrow by the 2-incidences. If the column values are $b_1,\ldots,b_m$, the frame outside an entry spanning columns $i$ through $j$ is
$$
                 l b_1\cdots b_{i-1},\qquad b_{j+1}\cdots b_m r .
$$
At a vertex use the prefix through it and the remaining suffix. At a point use the empty product. Full ordinary support makes the head traverse its entire outer spine. Apply the original head to the placed labels. Shared faces have the same two products. This proves by induction that the construction types each new declaration and refolds each raw term literally. Subdividing a spine and then placing an entry gives the same products as placing it after flattening. This is the substitution law, including empty or repeated factors. It involves no cancellation in $C$.

The same construction lifts converted terms and respects their equality. The retraction fixes the two words and their types. A word-head equation uses its whole supporting arity. The empty-factor and substitution formulas identify the placements of the restriction, the inserted type and each retained argument. Thus the corresponding equation applies to the placed tuple. Disc and endo removal use the same face placements. Induction on formation, congruence and conversion gives the family interpretation, with the original test on the bound arity for either coherence rule. Conversely, at an upstairs head its tuple of object copies and outside factors is forced by its exterior frame and the successive column values, reading prefixes from the left and suffixes from the right. Induction on the arguments then recovers that head at its original tuple. At a variable the assertion is the definition of its copy. These two inverses give bijections on raw terms and on surface classes at a prescribed frame, in particular on inhabitants at the canonical frame $(1,w,1)$ of an old type. The indexing category can be infinite. Every proposed converted derivation in the interval, its old boundary, and their dependencies already lie in a finite subcomputad. If the old type fails reflection downstairs this gives a finite counterexample upstairs, since any raw filler in that finite subcomputad refolds. For finite $C$, the entire interval is finite.

Take $F=1_{C_X}$. There is a directed path of generating edges from the terminal object copy $(y,w,1)$ back to the initial copy $(x,1,w)$ precisely when some edge word $v:y\to x$ satisfies $wv=1_x$ and $vw=1_y$ in $C_X$. Necessity reads the two exterior coordinates along the path. Conversely the successive prefixes and remaining suffixes of $v$ lift it from $(y,w,1)$ to $(x,1,w)$ using the two equations. If there is no return, colour the objects reachable from the terminal copy right and all others left. All arrows are nondecreasing, and the old query crosses the cut. If a return exists, its same word lifts in every category interval. Hence universal reflection for all queries with noninvertible path class reduces to reflection in finite computads at queries whose terminal object cannot reach their initial object. For the remaining queries the path class is invertible. Every object position of a placed derivation then lies over the strongly connected component of $x$ in the original graph, since it occurs on a path from $x$ to $y$ and there is a path back. One can also see this by lifting with $C_X$ and retaining only copies actually used, whose prefixes and suffixes have edge-word representatives. This is an exact reduction at individual old boundaries.

The two relations hold on **all** states because $ab$ and $abb$ act alike and $ba$ and $baa$ both act constantly dead. ### A strict test with the complexity of invertibility

Start with any finite category presentation $B$ by edges and path equations and a distinguished generating edge $f:x\to y$. Present the equations as directed 2-declarations at raw path writings. In the category of this enlarged 2-skeleton all four forward edges are equal, and eliminating the three new edge names recovers $B$.

This reverse has no raw or $A,U$ inhabitant for either directed coherence rule, independently of the path equations of $B$. Here is the needed support argument. Assign weight one to the original 2-declarations of $B$ and zero to the five 2-declarations of the square. The 3-declarations preserve this weight. In general, nonnegative weights on rank-$k$ generators preserved by all $(k+1)$-attachments define a strict interpretation in the $k$-fold delooping of $(\mathbb N,+)$, with only identities higher up. For $k=1$ use the ordinary one-object monoid category. Each factor of a strict composite of weight zero in rank at least $k$ has weight zero whenever its rank is at least $k$. Below cut $k$ composition adds; at higher cuts the two weights have to agree. Identity padding preserves weight from rank $k$ up. The same assertion holds for the faces of a labelled position by the attaching equations. Full ordinary support on the bound tree now shows that a zero-weight raw head application uses only zero-weight labels in these ranks, by evaluating a strict expression for the full cell and its typed support. This gives strengthening to the subcomputad of all lower generators and zero-weight higher generators. For binary conversion the proof by families above applies with a single upstairs entry over zero and an empty family over a positive value; when the output has weight zero all used inputs are present. Restrict a word equation to its support as before. Thus the strengthening also applies to any $A,U$ witness whose weight is forced to be zero by its old boundary. In the present example it restricts the proof to the original graph with just the square attachments. Now give every edge weight one. In the interval of width one every lifted edge crosses, including a lift of $f$ when $f$ was a loop. Interpret the forward declarations in the bare suspended square by the clamp of Response 22, other crossing edges by its first edge. The strict native reverse is forbidden there. This proves the assertion. For the surfaces containing $I$ the old reverse is inhabited by the square word law. [Square, clamp and strict soundness](#researcher-response-22) · [Edge interval](#researcher-response-26)

Use the admissible return-guarded $E'$ of Response 22. **On this family some category interval has no strict reverse if and only if $f$ is noninvertible in $B$.** If $f$ is invertible, the genuine return word lifts in every interval. The inner exchange in this hom is then strict under the return guard. Its two schedules have an identity witness, compatible with maps of intervals. If $f$ is noninvertible, use the universal interval and its cut. Map the left and right colours to the objects of the bare suspended square. Edges within a colour go to identities. Among crossing edges send $f_0,f_1,f_2,f_3$ to the successive bare-square edges with the two middle versions identified, and all original edges other than $f$ to the first bare edge. For an original 2-declaration both paths go to the same strict arrow. A crossing path uses exactly one crossing edge, and every original crossing edge maps to that first arrow. Give the declaration its identity there. A placed square entirely within one colour is constant; one across the cut uses the bare 2-cells, the stationary middle step and the two generating 3-updates, with strict unit whiskers at the compound attachment. This interprets every declaration, even if different placed copies of one original edge cross at different sites. The bare arity has the unchanged $E'$ table, so it excludes the strict reverse by requirement 3. The argument works in any interval without an object-graph return. Conversely a return makes the identity witness by the guard. Thus the test is exact on these inputs, even though their raw answer is constant.

For an explicit failure of every finite categorical test put loops $p,q$ at $x$, an edge $r:y\to x$, and impose $pq=1_x$, $qp=fr$, $rf=1_y$. In every finite category $pq=1_x$ in the finite endomorphism monoid implies $qp=1_x$ (multiplication with a one-sided inverse on a finite set is bijective). Thus $r$ gives a return in every finite interval. In the monoid $\langle P,Q\mid PQ=1\rangle$ assign $p,r$ to $P$ and $q,f$ to $Q$. Cancelling adjacent $PQ$ terminates and has no overlapping redex, giving the normal forms $Q^iP^j$. No word after a leading $Q$ cancels it. The terminal prefix therefore cannot return to the initial prefix. The cut above gives a strict obstruction in this interval. This strengthens the finite-sheet example to arbitrary finite categories with noninvertible arrows.

Finally the unrestricted strict-detection predicate on this family is $\Pi^0_1$-complete. An inverse word and its two finite equational derivations certify the complement. To see hardness, given a finite group presentation and words $u,v$, use its group at an object $T$ (include inverse edges and their relations). Add $a:Y\to T$, $b:T\to Y$, $f:Y\to Z$, $r:Z\to Y$ and impose
$$
                      a v b=1_Y,\qquad a u b=f r,\qquad r f=1_Z .
$$
If $u=v$, $f$ is invertible. Otherwise take $Y=\{0,1\}$, $Z=\{0\}$ and $T=G\times Y$, with the right regular action on the first coordinate. Send $a(y)$ to $(1,y)$ and let $b(g,y)$ be $y$ for $g=v$ and zero otherwise. Take the unique $f$ and the inclusion $r$. This satisfies the relations and $f$ has no inverse. Use a finitely presented group with many-one complete word problem. For instance the effective embedding of the countably generated recursively presented group with independent involutions $g_i$, killed exactly for $i$ in a complete c.e. set, gives such words in a finite presentation. [Word-problem construction](https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/journal-of-symbolic-logic/article/algorithmically-finite-universal-and-universal-groups/4E4FF5B43806F18C55CB7D526610F92C)

Detection using *some finite category* is recursively enumerable but undecidable. Enumerate finite composition tables and valid labellings, then check reachability in the finite interval graph. In the group construction, if a finite quotient distinguishes $u,v$, the displayed set model uses finite sets and gives a finite category witness. If none distinguishes them, every action of the presented group in a finite category has equal values at $u,v$, since it lands in the finite group of units at $T$. The actual word $r$ then returns in the interval. Deciding finite detection would decide whether each generator of a given finitely presented group can be distinguished from the identity in a finite quotient. Testing the finitely many generators would decide existence of a nontrivial finite quotient, contrary to Bridson–Wilton. These complexity statements classify the indicated strict test for actual old boundaries. Both raw and $A,U$ syntax reject the reverse throughout this family. [Finite quotient undecidability](https://arxiv.org/abs/1401.2273)

Fix a finite supply of individual names for copies of the generating 3-cells. First work in grades in which each name occurs at most once. Call a named cube outer if its marked component admits a representative with **all markers in one horizontal row**. At fixed lower boundaries form the cubical transition complex of these outer frames. At a vertex remember the subset of names already executed. An actual gap between wires has address equal to the number of lanes on its left. Its address stays constant in its planar face. Insert a site for each scalar name at each lane boundary, including the two exterior boundaries (one when the width is zero). Order the sites within a boundary arbitrarily for this proof. Give a scalar germ the single site of its address and name, together with its specified live cut. Against every exterior gate whose interval meets its site, its order is prescribed by that cut. For a clique of pairwise common-row signed germs, contract their specified disjoint positive expansions to rods and include the singleton sites. Orient comparisons of intersecting exterior gate intervals by their old lane order. Orient a box against each intersecting exterior gate using its specified single-germ frame, with the cut rule for a scalar. There are no interval intersections among the boxes. The intersection graph of these intervals is chordal. On every triangle the orders come from the original circuit or one single-germ frame. If two boxes were comparable, take two consecutive boxes on a comparison path. Its intervening exterior comparisons survive expanding the other boxes. The order is forced already in the pair frame by the port orders and, at its scalar ends, by the specified live cuts. That contradicts the existence of their common row. Thus the boxes are incomparable and one can schedule them all in one row after their strict predecessors. The component is unique: the port orders reconstruct the positive circuit and each scalar has its designated face there. The blockers of a scalar are exterior gates, since all the other boxes miss its site. Their order and the designated cut determine that face even after the contractions. The auxiliary ordering of sites does not select an ordering of points in any chart.

The signed-link argument of Response 10 now applies to this cubical complex. Its universal cover is CAT(0). Identity diagrams have the positive common-row calculation of Response 33 (equivalently the graph calculation of Response 21). On a divisor with the death and at most two events the outer graph has at most one edge between the two orders. Indeed there is at most one rod of each type, and simultaneous placement of the scalar alongside a given rod must use the other gap at vertex 1. Each singleton has only one placement there. Only $D$ itself supports all three events together. Its outer graph is the six-edge cycle and its entire fundamental group maps trivially to the full chart by the central-face hexagon above. This proves thinness everywhere it is required.

Stationary stacking preserves common rows and juxtaposed independent events have a common row. The stacked component still supplies the local Gray comparison. Thus the rectangle, naturality and exterior composition checks of Response 10 give the cartesian enrichment on these grades. Any equation at a high output holds in its single strict-label component. Apply the published and binary interpretations as in Response 33. The two-row evaluation, with the binary endpoint transports where appropriate, computes exactly the indicated fully faithful quotient on the low label. Any surface 5-filler implies equality of the original $F_2$ path classes and hence gives the raw filler at the written faces. Higher old parallel boundaries over this label on the 3-skeleton already have fillers by graph asphericity. This is a fixed-label result. It shows both why a direct extension of the common-row flag proof fails for one-sided lower generators and why that failure alone supplies no counterexample to Q3. [Evaluation at the written boundary](#reflection-at-the-written-boundary)

The solver enumerates typed serializations, closes them under adjacent lower exchanges, restricts each full chart to its actual pair charts and collapses private edges. It finds six outer edges, zero common-row triples and exactly one full hexagon with wholly outer boundary. It also checks every one- and two-marker placement on the four divisors containing the death. Agda 2.8.0 checks the declarations and the stationary diagram at their written types. **On every finite ordinary computad, the join in Q1(c) and full strict-image conversion inhabit exactly the same old types through dimension four. Even on the fixed 2-skeleton with one object and one scalar, recognition through degree four is $\Sigma^0_1$-complete. With unrestricted 2-skeleton it is neither computably enumerable nor co-computably enumerable.

### Testing the planar comparisons in the ambient context

Write $B=X_{\le2}$ and $J$ for the ordinary join. Use the decorated component types of Response 28. For every pair $D,E$ of floating components at the same exterior object, include their entire bounded-face contents and choose a grouped raw reading of the closed diagram $P(D,E)$. At that reading take the block half twist for identical types or the block full twist for different types, carrying the contents along without internal rotation. Denote this raw loop by $b$ and the identity at its 2-pole by $i$. The tests are the **two directed raw inhabitation questions in $X$**
\[
                         b\longrightarrow i,\qquad i\longrightarrow b.
\]
One fixed reading and either crossing convention suffice. The tests allow witnesses using the given higher attachments. In particular they do not prescribe the strict 4-value of a witness. [Planar component types and block motions](#researcher-response-28)

Passing these tests is equivalent to the following relative condition: **every ordered pair of parallel raw 3-terms of $B$ admits a raw 4-cell in $X$**. To see sufficiency, transport the two paths to the same grouped reading and take their difference loop. On the planar base the loop groups are generated by the sibling braids in each face, recursively through its contents. In a group of coloured braids the pure subgroup is generated by transported two-strand full twists, and the remaining permutations are generated by transported half twists of equal types. A full twist of equal siblings is the square of the half twist. A motion of a pair with its contents can be placed in the chosen gap of a grouped reading, with the surrounding diagram stationary. Whisker the ambient witness at that gap and transport it to the required reading by the raw base paths. Both directions are available. Composing with an inverse loop gives both comparisons for the inverse motion as well. Concatenation of the resulting comparisons deals with a product of generators. The groupoid presentation of the planar base realizes the necessary path homotopies by raw 4-cells, including all endpoint fits. This gives the comparison at the original written faces. The converse takes the indicated pair of parallel loops. By Response 6, $J$ identifies every such pair on $B$. Thus the tests are necessary for reflection. Let $H_X$ denote the free ordinary raw algebra and $C_B$ the strict 2-category on $B$ with higher identities, regarded as a raw algebra. Truncate the declarations of $X$ at degree four, which preserves judgments in these degrees by the arity bound. Form
\[
                           Z=H_X\amalg_{H_B} C_B .
\]
We first record why **every ordinary strict filler at old faces through degree four gives a raw filler in $Z$ at their images**, independently of the tests. Apply the strict-base construction $M$ of round-1 Response 31 with $r=2$ to this pushout. Its construction of $M(Z)$ uses two facts: $Z$ has the distinguished strict base through degree two, and for each *globular pasting arity* $P$ the generic extension
\[
                    H_P\amalg_{H_{P_{\le2}}} C_{P_{\le2}}\longrightarrow C_P

lifts at prescribed boundaries. The latter follows from the globular arity comparison on $P_{\le2}$, left properness and the gaunt-target test on $P$. Both facts hold here for every $B$. The additional weak-equivalence assumption on the actual $H_B\to C_B$ in that response is used to transfer a witness back from the pushout. Explicitly, $M(Z)$ keeps the literal lower cells of $Z$, takes 3-cells modulo mutual 4-reachability at fixed lower faces, and assigns a 4-cell to precisely the ordered pairs of classes admitting a witness. The strict operations on this truncation agree with evaluation of original raw terms followed by this projection. Hence the strict functor from the free strict category on $X$ to $M(Z)$ assigns the original classes to the original faces, even for nonglobular attachments. The resulting $H_P\to C_B$ has the globular boundary lifting property in every degree and is a trivial fibration. The auxiliary resolution can be infinite. ### Complexity on one scalar

Take $B=(x:*,a:1_x\to1_x)$, $w=a*_1 a$, and take $e=\mathrm{eh}(a,a)$, which realizes a single half twist with a fixed choice of sign. The whole test consists of $e\to1_w$ and $1_w\to e$. Searching simultaneously for both raw terms makes recognition computably enumerable on this fixed 2-skeleton, with arbitrary finite higher attachments. [Calculation of the single crossing](../answered/semistrict-surface-conservativity-2026-11-24.md#researcher-response-37)

Fix a finite presentation $G=\langle S\mid R\rangle$ whose identity word problem is many-one complete among computably enumerable sets. For every generator and its formal inverse attach a separate raw 3-endomorphism of $w$. Write $W(v)$ for a fixed bracketing of the word $v$ in these letters, with $W(\epsilon)=1_w$. Attach raw 4-variables in both directions between each relator word and $1_w$, and for cancellation in both orders of each inverse pair. Finally attach just two more variables in opposite directions between $e$ and $W(v)$. This is effective in $v$. If $v=1$ in $G$, a word derivation lifts in both directions using the declared relations. A relation step uses ordinary whiskering by its prefix and suffix, with raw temporal associators and unit comparisons at the two ends. The last two declarations therefore give both tests. [Nies and Sorbi, Theorem 3.2 and the reduction to the identity class](https://www.cs.auckland.ac.nz/~nies/papersnew/NiesSorbi_wordproblems.pdf)

For the converse there is a separating **braided strict monoidal groupoid**. Its objects are natural-number weights, with addition as tensor, and all arrows are endomorphisms. At weight two the group is $G$ and at every other weight the group is trivial. Tensor with the weight-zero unit preserves arrows. For two positive weights the tensor of arrows is the identity of their sum. This is a strict monoidal structure: in a tensor of several factors the only case with possibly nonidentity output has a single positive factor of weight two. Give the pair $(1,1)$ the braiding $[v]\in G$, or its inverse according to the sign of $e$, and every other pair the identity. Naturality for two positive inputs at total weight two only tests the two identity arrows at weight one. At larger positive totals the output group is trivial, and a unit input gives identity naturality. Both hexagons with three positive inputs likewise have trivial output, and with a zero input they reduce to unit laws. In particular $[v]$ need not be central.

Use the ordinary raw interpretation in the doubly degenerate Gray-category of this groupoid with only identity 4-cells. Interpret $a$ by weight one and each 3-letter by its element of $G$ at weight two. The value of $e$ is $[v]$ by the scalar crossing calculation, or equivalently by the braided functor from the free braided category on one object. Every 4-attachment has equal values at its two ends. If $[v]\ne1$, neither tested 4-cell can exist in this model. This proves the claimed completeness, also for the single fixed query $e\to1_w$ in the reduction. On arbitrary finite inputs, enumerating component pairs and searching for the two witnesses gives a $\Pi^0_2$ upper bound. In fact the unrestricted recognition problem is hard for differences of two computably enumerable sets: take a disjoint union of the one-scalar reduction for membership in the first set and the planar reduction for nonmembership in the second. Every component test and witness belongs to one summand, since arities are connected and both components have disjoint objects. Thus reflection holds exactly on the difference. ## Researcher Response 36

**If every generating 2-cell has a nonempty spatial input, each of the ten individual ordinary surfaces reflects old inhabitation through degree five, with arbitrary generators in degrees three and above. The same holds if every generating 2-cell has a nonempty spatial output.** In particular, a failure at degree five requires both an empty input and an empty output **among the 2-generators** of the finite dependency-closed presentation supporting it. A single 2-generator can satisfy both conditions. The common-row counterexample of Response 34 is in the scope of this theorem.

### Where a floating point can be kept

Use the common-input hypothesis. At rank three a generator on an empty spatial input has empty output and its two strict 2-faces are the identity on that empty path. An ordinary lower gate cannot act on an empty input word. Thus the lower marker circuits consist of rods (including rods with empty output), ordinary input-consuming gates and individually named scalar points. After forgetting the points, every nonempty component is anchored at the input boundary. The fixed named grades have the full chart presentation on fundamental groupoids of Response 33: the empty and unary coefficient components are contractible by anchoring, planar asphericity and the one-point face calculation. A binary chart still contributes one exchange per component, even if its two markers are ineligible for identification below. [One-sided rod diagrams](#researcher-response-14) · [Fundamental groupoid with floating points](#the-named-exchanges-that-can-be-identified)

Each complementary face of an input-anchored positive-input circuit has a **unique forward end**, or sink. It is either a particular gap on the output boundary or a particular strictly interior gap of the input ports of a gate. To prove this, follow an actual gap in a serialization. At a gate of nonzero output the two side gaps continue on the two sides, interior input gaps end, and interior output gaps begin. At a gate of zero output the two side gaps join and continue through the single gap above the gate. Since no gate has empty input, no gap branches going upwards. The finite graph of these continuations has a unique end on each connected component: every gap has one successor or is an end, and the row index increases along a successor. Its connected components are precisely the planar faces, by gluing the open strips between consecutive gate rows. The end has an intrinsic address in the port diagram, invariant under interchanges of disjoint steps. In particular the two lower gaps which join above a death belong to the same face. The address at a gate includes the gap index, since different interior gaps can end at that gate.

In a marked circuit use the following cubes for endpoint identification.

1. The rods alone can be exposed in one horizontal row in the circuit with scalar points forgotten.
2. The sink of every marked scalar face is an output gap or belongs to an **ordinary** gate of the marked circuit.

Multiple named points can occupy the same face. Equivalently one may put them in a small neighbourhood immediately before their sink, avoiding all marked rod rectangles. This specifies their joint component since finite configurations of named points in a face are connected. Expanding any of the rods to either attaching diagram preserves every such exterior end. A point near an exterior gate or output is still at that very gap after the expansion. A rod face remains a common-row rod frame by Response 14. Consequently these cubes and their temporal faces form a cubical transition complex on the vertices with executed-name subsets of Response 33. Every genuine common-row cube belongs to it: a scalar in the row starts in a gap outside the rod inputs, follows a side or an unaffected gap past the row, and cannot end inside the input ports of a rod already passed. In Response 34 the scalar ending at $a|d$ and the two rods give an additional eligible cube. Its temporal hexagon fills the outer cycle there.

### Signed links and composition

Fix a vertex with ordinary diagram $P$. A signed scalar germ is its unused or executed name, according to the sign, and a particular end of $P$. For one rod germ its contraction leaves all ordinary gates outside its expanded subdiagram, with their concrete incidences in the new circuit and the same port indices. The eligible pair with that scalar germ exists exactly when the end is an output gap or its gate is outside the rod expansion. Indeed every eligible sink in the contracted circuit comes from exactly such a surviving address, and placing the point by that sink realizes the prescribed expanded face. The assertion includes idle wires and a rod with an empty output. An interior input gap of the rod itself would give a different marked chart.

In a clique the rod germs have a unique joint common-row frame by Response 14. Each prescribed scalar end survives *every* one of their expansions by the pair test. Their expansions in the joint frame occupy disjoint input hulls and disjoint subdiagrams of $P$. Hence its gate, if any, is exterior to their union. Place each scalar at its surviving sink in the joint frame. These choices specify exactly one chart component, also for coincident sinks and for several copies distinguished by name. This proves existence and uniqueness of the cube for the entire clique. Conversely prescribed faces of such a cube give exactly these germs. The executed-name subset prevents any cube from using one name twice. Thus the signed links are simplicial and flag; there is no requirement that the points themselves share the rod row. Applying the CAT(0) directed-interval argument of Response 10 gives thinness of the subgroupoid generated by eligible exchanges in the **full** fundamental groupoid. Its cube-chain contractions use genuine full-chart relations, because every eligible cube is an actual marked circuit component. [Common-row rod link proof](#the-outer-signed-links) · [Directed interval contraction](#which-exchanges-can-be-made-identities)

Whiskering by a stationary planar diagram preserves eligibility. When stacking below, an interior sink or output is retained. When stacking above, an old output gap continues until it reaches the new output or a gate of the stationary diagram. The rod row is still available in either case. Under juxtaposition an interior end retains its gate and an output gap retains its position shifted by the opposite output width when appropriate. A cross-factor pair can have a common row and is eligible, including at empty spatial paths. These facts prove exactly the stability and strict exterior interchange conditions used in Response 33. The local Gray interchanger continues to use its actual stacked two-marker component. The squares, three-marker hexagons and the four-marker juxtaposed array in the full chart give the required laws, with endpoint transports along the eligible edges. Thus the temporary-name enriched model is fully faithful on tracks in every multilinear grade, with the single-object extension per repeated-name strict label as there.

Use the occurrence-family construction of Response 33 to restore arbitrary original multiplicities. It retains the endpoint bijection and the track on distinct names in one coordinate. The binary normal evaluation, and separately the published evaluation, realize the named chart paths at their prescribed raw boundaries. The empty-change comparison on diagrams is the input-anchored comparison of Response 14. Hence the rank-three essential surjectivity and the prescribed lifts at ranks four and five give the same high completion and arbitrary higher base change as in Responses 11, 27 and 33. For the common-output hypothesis read the **lower circuits** from the output boundary and use the unique backward end of each face. Each face expansion and its circuit whiskerings obey the reversed calculation. The temporal direction of attached 3-cells and the direction of higher declarations are preserved. Response 32 consequently shifts either one-sided criterion to degree six in its indexed crossing hom. [Occurrence assembly](#assembly-at-arbitrary-original-multiplicities) · [Higher declarations](#researcher-response-11) · [Degree shift](#researcher-response-32)

The first solver computes face components by gluing both sides of every actual gate row and checks their ends independently against all signed expansions. It enumerates actual rod common slices on circuits with splits, merges, whole-word deaths and a change that deletes opposite ends of a three-wire input on its two faces. With five inputs through three ordinary or rod gates and two named points, it finds 39,386 edges, 185,014 two-corners, 257,800 three-corners, 51,376 four-corners and 192 five-corners. Each clique has exactly one eligible frame. In 7,316 signed face expansions a face ending at a marker joins a retained face, which illustrates why identifying all scalar placements over the expanded face would be wrong. The second solver independently locates the additional three-marker relation in the typed example of Response 34.
