## Researcher Response 75

**Arbitrary free 3-scalar probes preserve the six reflection results on the entire spanning-port class of [Response 73](#researcher-response-73).** There is a more general reduction for disconnected ports. Require each 2-face of a structural 3-rule to contain at least one generating gate. On a wired face, trace those spatial wires that run from the input rim to the output rim without encountering any gate. None may join the first input to the first output or the last input to the last output, in spatial order. An interior through wire is allowed, and no two gates need be connected. A point port has no extra condition. Apply the test on both sides of each use. Free probes are the independent 3-scalars of [Response 67](#researcher-response-67).

For the two published surfaces and the four binary surfaces omitting interchange, a surface witness reduces to its unmarked query. For each of the four binary surfaces containing interchange, it suffices in degree \(n\ge5\) to test the unmarked query and one-object scalar rules with nonempty ports and at most \(n-4\) probe occurrences at a time. Uniformly over this port class these are equivalent tests, since both subclasses occur in it. At a fixed unmarked history, every connected one-probe space is aspherical with free fundamental group, and its map to the all-gate flattening is injective on fundamental groups. In particular on Response 73's class, *all ten* reflect in degree at least six at histories with at most one probe in each spacetime component. The count is per component over the whole history, not per face of a single drawing.

### When two accesses to a site remain distinct

Use the forgetful fibre and batch isotopy of [Responses 66](#researcher-response-66) and [67](#researcher-response-67). Fix a serial representative. Erase wires while keeping every gate point, signed seam and moving site disk. This includes the actual open one-probe spacetime in the flat one. First consider one adjacent cut and the disk of its site, split at a collared circle meeting its rim wires transversely. The flat cut is the union of the punctured disk and the punctured exterior along that circle. The circle injects in both fundamental groups because the disk contains a gate. In the actual cut use the faces of the two halves and glue along the accessible rim intervals, or along the whole rim at a point site.

Each half-face injects on fundamental groups in the corresponding flat half. Indeed each bounded complementary component of that planar face contains a gate point, or contains the excised disk on the exterior side. To see this, a finite component of the removed wire graph disjoint from both the boundary and the gates would have to be a closed gate-free wire, impossible by progression in drawing height. Boundary-attached graph joins the complement across that boundary. Pick one retained puncture per bounded hole (a point in the excised disk suffices for its hole). Filling everything except the chosen punctures maps the face's disjoint access meridians to a free basis. This proves injectivity, also for faces disjoint from the rim.

There is a stronger statement about paths. Suppose a path in a half-face with endpoints on the accessible rim becomes homotopic relative to its ends to a rim path in the flat half. It is already homotopic in the face to an accessible rim path. For a point site the whole rim is available and half-face injectivity proves this. For a wired site take an arm from each rim end to its first gate, or the entire chord for a through wire. In the exterior an arm can instead escape to the outer boundary. No exterior wire can run directly between two rim ends: after contracting the site such a wire would go forward in drawing height from the marker back to itself. The arms are proper arcs in the corresponding flat half, with distinct coordinates for different arcs even if they end at the same puncture. Intersection numbers with each oriented arc are invariant under a path homotopy fixing the two endpoints off the arms. The actual path has zero intersection. On pushing a rim traversal just inside the half, a single-ended arm contributes one signed unit; a through chord contributes opposite units at its two ends.

Read the rim ends in cyclic order, the bottom in spatial order and the top in reverse. A traversal winding at least one full turn in either direction meets every single-ended arm with the same sign on each visit. At least one such arm exists under the stated condition: a nonempty noncrossing perfect matching on the circle has a cyclically adjacent matched pair. A shorter, nonempty sequence of crossed ends could have total vector zero only if they were all matched in pairs within that consecutive sequence. The endpoints of an innermost pair would then be adjacent. A through chord joins bottom to top, so the only possible adjacent pairs are precisely the two extreme pairs in the criterion. In the exterior all arms are single-ended. It follows on either side that a zero-vector rim path reduces to a path in one accessible interval. Half-face injectivity now gives the required homotopy there. Conversely an adjacent matched pair gives a rim traversal crossing precisely its two ends with zero vector, including the full-turn case on two ends. This converse concerns the access test; the calculation below distinguishes actual outcomes when it fails.

### From local paths to the configuration fibre

Here is how to use this relative path statement when several tie faces continue into the *same* cut face. Split the cut into halves as above. The incidence graph has vertex spaces the half-faces and edges the rim intervals or circles. Edge groups inject in vertex groups, including at a point site by the nontrivial flat peripheral. In the universal cover the incidence graph is a tree, by the amalgam normal form. Map this tree to the flat cut tree. Two incident edges at a lifted half mapping to the same edge would give a half-face path between their rim positions flattening to a rim path. The relative path statement identifies the two lifted edges already. The tree map is therefore locally injective and embeds, and its vertex stabilizers inject by half-face injectivity. In particular if a cut path with endpoints in exterior half-faces can be homotoped into the *flat* exterior, its lifted endpoints are on one flat exterior vertex. Their actual exterior vertices coincide by tree injectivity. The path can consequently be homotoped into that actual exterior half-face.

Next split the spacetime at the event times. The vertex spaces are cut faces and each temporal edge space is an exterior half-face at the corresponding tie. Seams transport the chosen positions. All edge groups inject: in the flat cut this follows from the disk peripheral, and actual exterior groups inject even after flattening. The cut path statement just proved is the no-fold condition at a temporal vertex, separately for the preceding and following ties (their images are different temporal directions). Thus each actual temporal tree embeds in the flat temporal tree and cut stabilizers inject. The resulting map on one-probe fundamental groups is injective, and both spaces are aspherical since their trees of spaces have contractible lifted vertices and edges. This proof also covers components that meet neither end of the history.

The flat fundamental group is free. In serial order a tie is a plane punctured at the stationary gates and one site marker. On passing to the next cut, the marker maps to the peripheral product of its nonempty output packet; choose the same access stems for the stationary gates. This product belongs to a free basis together with the stationary generators and all but one of the packet generators. Thus gluing the next cut adds a free factor of rank one less than the packet size. Starting with the initial punctures gives rank equal to the initial gate count plus the sum of those increments. The original component groups are subgroups of this free group.

For clarity, group injectivity here suffices in place of a retraction of one-probe spaces. For a connected actual component and its flat target take their contractible universal covers. Distinct lifts of the fixed named punctures remain distinct by fundamental-group injectivity. Puncturing each cover at all lifts leaves it simply connected by general position. By excision its reduced homology is free in degree two on the individual meridian spheres and vanishes elsewhere. Joining the spheres to a basepoint gives a wedge equivalence by the homology form of Whitehead's theorem. Inclusion sends each sphere to its own meridian with the induced orientation. The pinch onto the corresponding wedge summands splits this map up to based homotopy. Thus punctured component inclusions inject on all positive groups, including the fundamental group from before puncturing. Use the forget-last-point fibration and its proper-ray section as in [Response 63](#researcher-response-63). Induction gives injectivity on all positive groups for ordered configurations in one component. If several components are occupied, forget the probes in all but one at a time. The degree-wise coordinate bound is that of the same response.

Given a surface witness, erase the probes. Once its unmarked difference fills raw, lift the original difference to the fibre by exactness. The occurrence lifts and coordinate specializations of [Response 69](#researcher-response-69) test this fibre element in the flat total spaces. The flat initial-collar section there ensures that vanishing in those totals implies vanishing in the corresponding flat fibres. The injections just proved then give raw vanishing. The six surfaces pass the flat tests by [Response 48](#researcher-response-48). With one name per occupied component the fibre itself has zero higher groups by asphericity, giving the stronger degree-six-and-above conclusion on an unmarked class with trivial higher groups.

### One event for which the access test fails

Take parallel wires across the entire site, place nonempty lists of unary gates on them on both sides of a single event, and put a probe in an interior gap. Before and after the event its strip is simply connected. At the tie the site spans the strip, leaving two accessible ends in drawing height. The spacetime graph for this component has two vertices and two interval edges, hence fundamental group infinite cyclic. Its generator passes the input gates on the gap side and returns past the output gates. This component reaches an end, so the collar section of Response 69 detects its generator in the raw total too.

**Flattening detects that generator exactly when the union of the occupied gate lanes on both ports meets both sides of the gap.** To calculate it, push the probe through each positioned list, then return along the same exterior reference side of the whole packet. Identify the two packet peripherals in the flat amalgam and divide the two resulting words. In abelianization the comb for a port counts once, with a common sign, precisely the gates on one side of the gap. The sole relation identifies the sum of the input meridians with the sum of the output meridians. The difference vanishes only if both lists count nothing or both count everything. In the first case the two combs are already trivial; in the second they both traverse the whole packet peripheral with the same orientation. Hence vanishing in these cases holds for the actual words. For instance a two-wire site with its only gate on wire 1 on each port has an invisible raw generator in the middle gap (wires numbered from 0). Moving the output gate to wire 0 detects it, although neither port alone has gates on both sides. An invisible generator limits this flattening test; its surface filling is a separate question.

**Reproduce.** Run `python3 tools/semistrict_surface_rim_access.py --agda-dir DIR` and `agda --safe --no-libraries -i src -i DIR DIR/RimAccess.agda` with an existing directory. The solver compares the extremal-wire test against equality of rim prefix vectors modulo the whole-turn vector. It accepts 3,234 wired ports, including 1,198 with through wires, and replays 5,824 collisions. On strip inputs it calculates actual positioned braid words, compares them after the peripheral identification, and independently checks the occupied-lane criterion on 642 detected and 140 invisible cases, plus repeated and reordered gates. Passing a JSON file with `{"width":2,"input":[1],"output":[0],"gap":1}` returns the surviving word. The rule JSON format of [Response 73](#researcher-response-73) tests both written faces. Agda checks the disconnected two-gate endomorphism and a full-width change composed with a probe in its padded interior gap. [Rim-access and strip solver](../../../tools/semistrict_surface_rim_access.py)

## Researcher Response 76

**There is a computable free rank for the one-probe space of every finite history with nonempty structural ports, without any restriction on through wires.** More generally, some empty ports are allowed. Choose a component of the cut-and-tie incidence graph of [Response 61](#researcher-response-61). Choose a gap in its time interval and sweep away from it in both directions. At a point site, if the joint face surrounding the site belongs to the component, require the port on the *new* side to contain a gate. Wired sites have no condition, even when their port is an identity diagram. If such a root gap exists, this component has the homotopy type of a finite graph. With \(v\) running over its cut faces, \(e\) over its tie faces and \(h\) the number of directly enclosed floating children of that face, its free rank is
\[
 r=1+\sum_v(h_v-1)+\sum_e(1-h_e).
\]
One may choose a different root for each component of a single serial refinement of a concurrent history. In particular a death followed by a birth can pass at their common empty cut. At an allocation with at most one probe per rooted occupied component, a degree-six-or-higher surface test reduces to its unmarked query, on **all ten** ordinary surfaces. This uses no assumption about detection by all-gate flattening.

### Why a sweep adds only graph cells

Split a new cut face at the rim of the site disk as in [Response 75](#researcher-response-75). At a wired site the overlaps are disjoint intervals. Each inside half-face has finite graph type, and the whole cut face is therefore built up to homotopy from its disjoint exterior half-faces by adding vertices and edges. For example, replace the inside half-faces by graph spines and each gluing interval by one edge in the double mapping cylinder. This works when several different exterior half-faces join into a single cut face. At a point site the exterior half-face surrounding the disk attaches to the outer face of a punctured or wired port along a circle. That outer face has one meridian per outermost floating child. When the port is nonempty, there is at least one child; the rim peripheral is the product of those meridians in a suitable access order. It is a member of a free basis, by replacing one meridian with the product. Thus the inner outer face has a graph model *relative to the rim circle* consisting of that circle with more loops attached. One can use a map from this wedge extending the given rim map and representing the remaining basis elements; it is a homotopy equivalence between aspherical spaces. Faces away from the rim attach independently. If the new point port is empty, the corresponding rim bounds a disk, which is the excluded case for the incident component.

In the temporal homotopy pushout the exterior pieces are the tie faces. The relative models just constructed attach the new cut by graph cells even when a map into the previously processed cut kills a loop. Start with all component faces of the root gap; each has graph type. Repeat the pushouts to the two ends. Extra components may start along the way and are joined according to the actual incidence maps. This proves the graph type. Additivity of Euler characteristic in these same finite pushouts gives the formula. To find the root interval, every marked tie with empty left port imposes root gap at or before that event's left gap, and every marked tie with empty right port imposes root gap at or after its right gap. Intersect these bounds with the component's gap interval. The exact sequence for erasing probes gives the stated reflection implication since a product of one-probe graph spaces has vanishing higher groups. Failure of the root test by itself leaves the type undecided.

When both the actual component and the flat space pass the root test, there is also an exact **fixed-history decision** whether the latter detects its whole one-probe fundamental group. Calculate the image subgroup rank and compare with \(r\). For effectivity in arbitrary positioned movies one can triangulate the polyhedral pair as in [Response 69](#researcher-response-69), taking a common barycentric subdivision of the obstacle pair and the outside box. The allowed face flags give finite complement models with the induced inclusion. Maximal trees give finite presentations and generator images. Enumerate presentation isomorphisms to free presentations of the computed ranks, with inverse maps and ordinary relator proofs on the finite generator lists. This terminates by the graph-type result. Fold the resulting flat words as labelled loops based at a common vertex. The rank of the folded graph is the image rank. Equality with \(r\) is equivalent to injectivity: a surjection between finite free groups of the same rank is an isomorphism (equivalently no rank-lowering fold can occur in folding a free basis). If injective, Response 75 gives the comparison for all ordered configurations in that component and the same reduction to the unmarked and flat surface tests. The executable word calculation below implements the strip family directly. [Stallings, *Topology of finite graphs*](https://www2.math.ou.edu/~nbrady/teaching/s11-5863/stallings.pdf)

### Exact kernels across any number of strip changes

Fix an interior spatial gap in the full-width unary strip of Response 75. Take \(m\) full-width changes and nonempty lists of gates at its \(m+1\) cuts; repeated lanes and different reading orders are allowed. Use the specified common reading at each intervening cut. Mark each cut as left-only, right-only, or mixed. Give each mixed cut its *own* identifier, even when two cut lists are identical. Let \(d\) count the resulting distinct identifiers, including the left-only and right-only identifiers that actually occur. Then the raw one-probe rank is \(m\), the flat image rank is \(d-1\), and flattening is faithful exactly when neither single-side identifier occurs twice. In the consecutive loop basis \(u_1,\ldots,u_m\), normal generators of the kernel are the products \(u_{j+1}\cdots u_i\) from the first occurrence \(j\) of each repeated identifier to each subsequent occurrence \(i\).

Indeed, the actual space has a simply connected strip at each cut and two tie intervals per change. Use the lower tie edge to transport basepoints along the history. Crossing the input cut to the upper edge and coming back through the output cut gives the consecutive free basis. In the flat space choose the same exterior reference side of the packet throughout. The corresponding reference paths through a tie transport to the two adjacent cuts, so the image of \(u_i\) is \(c_{i-1}c_i^{-1}\), where \(c_i\) is the positioned gap comb followed by the inverse reference arc at cut \(i\). Van Kampen identifies the full packet peripheral \(z\) at all cuts. Thus the flat group is the free product of the cut groups amalgamated along their common primitive cyclic subgroup.

A single-side comb is either trivial or the same oriented full peripheral, with opposite side types giving the two different values. At a mixed cut, the abelianization of the comb counts a proper nonempty subset of the gates. It is independent of the total peripheral vector, so the comb and \(z\) do not commute (commuting elements of a free group are powers of a common element). They freely generate a rank-two subgroup. The amalgam normal form now says that all mixed combs and \(z\) together form a free basis of their subgroup: each two-generator subgroup meets the other cut groups along the amalgamated cyclic factor. In this basis the differences from one fixed comb to all the other distinct combs are freely independent. If a comb is trivial choose it as the reference; otherwise replace each other basis letter by its difference from the chosen one. The products \(u_1\cdots u_i\) are a basis of the raw ladder group and map to exactly these differences, with repetitions and the identity for recurrences of the initial comb. Identifying the repeated products and killing those identities proves the kernel formula.

For width two, cuts \([1],[0,1],[1]\) in the middle gap give consecutive images \(z^{-1}b,b^{-1}z\). Both survive and their product dies. This gives a **raw degree-five nonfilling** since the component reaches an end and its collar section injects the fibre. There is a higher example on the same movie with two probes. For any graph-type orientable one-probe component with group \(G\), forgetting the second probe has aspherical base and fibre the component punctured at the first probe. Its universal cover is contractible punctured at the \(G\)-orbit of the first probe. Thus \(\pi_2\) of the two-probe configuration is free abelian on the meridian spheres indexed by \(G\). With compatible lifts, flattening to another graph-type component maps the sphere indexed by \(g\) to that indexed by its image. In this example the difference of the spheres indexed by \(1\) and \(u_1u_2\) is a nonzero degree-six class that dies in the flat test. The end collar again detects it in the original raw total. Neither calculation alone decides an interchange-surface filler.

**Reproduce.** Run `python3 tools/semistrict_surface_probe_sweep.py --agda-dir DIR` and `agda --safe --no-libraries -i src -i DIR DIR/ProbeSweep.agda` with an existing directory. The solver checks the root intervals on every sequence of two to five scalar cuts of sizes zero through two, including 188 admitting a root. A separate cellular complement calculation gives Betti numbers \((1,2,0,0)\) for the two-crossbar strip. Carrying a stationary puncture through its upper tie faces gives free rank three. Across 3,900 strip histories the solver folds the actual positioned images, compares their rank with the identifier count, and evaluates every proposed kernel word. Of these, 2,120 are faithful, 816 have a kernel even though every individual change is detected, and 964 kill an individual generator. Agda checks the two consecutive changes at the written right-only, mixed and right-only 2-faces. Pass `{"width":2,"gap":1,"cuts":[[1],[0,1],[1]]}` in a JSON file to obtain the words and kernel generators. The serial movie JSON of Response 61 returns free ranks and root intervals on general cut and tie faces, including adjacent uses with disjoint gate support.
